● Update(README.md)
  ⎿  Added 1 line, removed 1 line
      16  - If an event_id arrives again with a different body, the first
           body stands. The repeat gets a 200 and is logged.
      17  - Intake answers 200 only after Postgres has committed the even
          t. It answers 400 when the body is not a JSON object, or when e
          vent_id, user_id or created_at is missing, empty or unreadable,
           and 413 when the body is over 1 MB. `type` and `data` pass thr
          ough unchecked. Any database error gets a 503, which makes no p
          romise, and every 400 is logged with its reason.
      18  - Any 2xx from the endpoint means saved. Any other status, a re
          fused connection or a failed connect means not saved, and the e
          vent is retried after 20 ms, doubling on each failure in a row
          up to 5 seconds. Redirects are not followed. An event the endpo
          int keeps refusing blocks its user; each failure is logged at e
          rror level with a count, and a status line every minute shows u
          nsent events, the oldest one's age, the longest send in flight
          and how many users are blocked.
      19 -- A slow answer is always waited for, and an endpoint that neve
         -r answers is outside the brief's table. Sends have no overall t
         -imeout, only a 2-second connect timeout; a send in flight over 
         -60 seconds is logged as a warning with its event_id. TCP keepal
         -ive (the system checking an idle connection's other end is stil
         -l there) makes a connection whose other end has vanished fail. 
         -That failure, like any connection lost after the request went o
         -ut and before a status line came back, means the outcome is unk
         -nown, and the event is sent again. The status line alone decide
         -s: a 200 means saved even if the rest of the answer is cut off.
      19 +- A slow answer is always waited for, and an endpoint that neve
         +r answers is outside the brief's table. Sends have no overall t
         +imeout, only a 2-second connect timeout; a send in flight over 
         +60 seconds is logged as a warning with its event_id. TCP keepal
         +ive (the system checking an idle connection's other end is stil
         +l there) makes a connection whose other end has vanished fail. 
         +That failure, like any connection lost after the request went o
         +ut and before a status line came back, is the brief's "No conne
         +ction: We are down. Not saved.", and the event is sent again. T
         +he status line alone decides: a 200 means saved even if the res
         +t of the answer is cut off.                                    
      20  - On a normal stop the worker starts no new sends and waits up
          to 120 seconds for the ones in progress, exiting as soon as the
          y finish. The stop signal never cancels a send in progress or i
          ts `delivered_at` write. A send still going after that, or one
          cut short by a hard kill, is sent again on restart and may be s
          aved twice.
      21  - A 429 pauses only the user whose request got it, since the br
          ief does not say whether the limit is per user or for the whole
           endpoint, and a per-user pause works under both. The pause las
          ts its Retry-After seconds, and at least 1 second, which also c
          overs a missing header, a value that is not a whole number and
          `Retry-After: 0`. A 429 does not raise the retry wait.
      22  - Tequity starts sending once `docker compose up` shows our ser
          vices running. Posts in the first few seconds may be refused, a
          nd a refused post carries no 2xx promise. Postgres keeps its da
          ta in a named volume, so stored events survive `docker compose
          down` and `up`. Only `down -v` deletes them, and a schema chang
          e needs it, since the init SQL runs only on an empty data folde
          r. The API and worker never exit on a database error; they log
          it and retry, and a panic in one user's loop is caught and logg
          ed without stopping the others.

● Update(docs\decisions.md)
  ⎿  Added 2 lines
      266 
      267  `depends_on` only orders `docker compose up`. If Postgres rest
           arts later on its own, the API and worker get no such wait. So
            neither process ever exits on a database error. At startup th
           ey retry the connection until Postgres answers, and while runn
           ing they log each database error and retry inside the process:
            intake answers 503 meanwhile, and the worker holds its loops.
            Each user loop in the worker recovers from a panic, logs it w
           ith the user_id and event_id, and counts it as a failed attemp
           t, so the retry wait applies. A panic in one user's loop would
            otherwise kill the whole process, and the restart would hit t
           he same row and crash again, stopping every user. `restart: un
           less-stopped` stays as the last line of defence. (Added 2026-1
           1-19 after review.)
      268 
      269 +An event we refuse at intake, with a connection refused during
          + startup or a 503 during a Postgres restart, carries no promis
          +e: the brief's promise starts with our 2xx. If Tequity sends i
          +t again after that user's later events have already gone out, 
          +it arrives out of order. We assume that is outside the pass co
          +nditions, since we never answered 2xx for it until after its l
          +ater events. (Added 2026-11-19 after review.)                 
      270 +                                                              
      271  `docker compose up` starts containers in parallel, and Tequity
           's load generator could post before Postgres or the API is rea
           dy. The README assumes Tequity starts sending once our service
           s show as running. A post refused in the first few seconds got
            no 2xx, so it carries no promise.

          and why. One decision per commit. The commit message states the

      273  ## 2026-11-19: Every text file is checked out with LF line end

      274 
      275  The repo has a `.gitattributes` with `* text=auto eol=lf`, so
           every text file has LF line endings in every checkout, Windows
            included; the PDF is detected as binary and left alone. Git o
           n the Windows laptop this is built on has `core.autocrlf=true`
           . Without the file, a checkout there turns line endings into C
           RLF, so a Docker build from that laptop could copy files into
           the image that differ from what the graders build on Linux, an
           d a shell script with CRLF fails outright on Linux. What is co
           mmitted was already LF (`git ls-files --eol` showed `i/lf` for
            each text file), so nothing in the history changes. The repo
           keeps no shell scripts beyond what compose and the Dockerfile
           need inline; the init SQL is the only file Postgres runs. (Add
           ed after review.)
      276 +                                                              
      277 +## 2026-11-19: Before handover, the repo is run from a fresh c
          +lone on Linux                                                 
      278 +                                                              
      279 +Before the repo goes to Tequity, it is run the way the graders
          + run it, on a Linux machine with plain Docker Engine: `git clo
          +ne`, then `docker compose up`, then the test rig from `rig/com
          +pose.yaml`. Docker Desktop on Windows, where this is built, hi
          +des differences that matter on Linux: file modes, line endings
          +, the case of file names, and host networking itself, which th
          +ere runs inside a VM. It also never runs a firewall in front o
          +f the containers. This run is also where the 127.0.0.1 case fr
          +om the host networking entry gets proven. A GitHub Actions Ubu
          +ntu runner is one way to get such a machine; it needs the repo
          + pushed to GitHub, which is decided when we get there. The REA

● I worked through all 25 items in file order, one commit each, plus one extra
  commit to fix a format slip of mine (26 in all). Nothing was reset or amended.
  Only README.md, docs/decisions.md, CLAUDE.md and the new .gitattributes were
  staged; the review file and the chat export are untouched.

  What I measured for item 3

  - Our time per event: I timed the worker's database steps with pgbench
    (Postgres's own load tool) against a throwaway Postgres on host networking
    at 127.0.0.1:54329, with 30,000 unsent events loaded.
    - Marking an event delivered and finding the user's next one took about 3.6
      ms on 1 connection and 4.7 ms on 20.
    - My earlier guess was 2 ms. The maths now uses 5 ms.
  - The poll query (item 10): with the proposed index it never touches the table
    rows and took 1.8 ms.
    - pgbench had marked most events delivered by then, so that was with 1,000
      unsent, not 30,000.
    - At 30,000 it's an estimate, about 25 to 30 ms, well inside the 200 ms
      between polls.
  - I removed the throwaway Postgres afterwards.

  The per-user maths, redone

  - The worst case: the heaviest user, taken as 30 a second, after a 5-minute
    outage.
    - It owes 18,000 events within 5 minutes of recovery.
    - Recovery can be noticed up to 5 seconds late, which leaves 295 seconds.
    - So each event must take 16.4 ms or less, start to finish.
  - Slowest endpoint answer that still meets that:

  ┌────────────────────────┬────────────────────────┬───────────────────────┐
  │   Share of attempts    │ First retry after 0.5  │ First retry after 20  │
  │      answered 500      │        s (old)         │       ms (now)        │
  ├────────────────────────┼────────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ 0%                     │ 11.4 ms                │ 11.4 ms               │
  ├────────────────────────┼────────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ 2%                     │ under 1 ms             │ 10.7 ms               │
  ├────────────────────────┼────────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ 10%                    │ not possible           │ 8.0 ms                │
  └────────────────────────┴────────────────────────┴───────────────────────┘

  - Result: the design is now sized for an endpoint that answers in about 10 ms,
    down from 14 ms. A user sending 50 a second would need about 4.2 ms.
  - One way to win back time: commit delivered_at while the user's next event is
    already being sent, since the 200 has already confirmed the save. That
    takes our 5 ms almost entirely off each event. I haven't decided it; it
    needs your yes.

● Update(docs\decisions.md)
  ⎿  Added 2 lines
      86 
      87  After a 2xx the event is saved, whatever happens next on our si
          de. If the `UPDATE ... SET delivered_at` fails, the loop retrie
          s only that UPDATE until it commits, and never sends the event
          again because of a database error. The user's loop waits until
          the commit goes through. Only a crash before that commit leads
          to a resend. (Added 2026-11-19 after review.)
      88 
      89 +The `delivered_at` commit for one event finishes before the use
         +r's next event is sent, never at the same time. If they overlap
         +ped, a crash after event 2 was sent but before event 1's commit
         + went through would send event 1 again on restart, after event 
         +2: a double save and an order break in one. Committing in paral
         +lel would take our 5 ms per event off the heavy user's time, an
         +d was dropped for that reason. (Added 2026-11-19.)             
      90 +                                                               
      91  Connection pools are set by hand, since Go's defaults do not fi
          t. Go's database pool has no limit by default, and Postgres all
          ows 100 connections, so 200 user loops could take them all and
          leave intake unable to commit. The worker's pool is capped at 2
          0 connections and the API's at 30, 50 in all. Each user loop ho
          lds a connection only for one statement at a time. pgbench with
           20 connections ran about 3,460 worker steps a second (mark del
          ivered, then look up the next event), 17 times the 200 a second
           the outage maths needs. Go's HTTP client keeps only 2 idle con
          nections per host by default, so most of 200 sends at once woul
          d open a new TCP connection each time; the worker sets `MaxIdle
          ConnsPerHost` to 200. (Added 2026-11-19 after review.)
      92 
      93  The worker keeps in memory which users are in flight, plus any
          pause or retry wait. None of that has to survive a restart. Aft
          er one, anything without `delivered_at` is sent again, and the
          worst a forgotten wait costs is one extra 500 or 429.

● Write(README.md)

       2 
       3  ## Assumptions
       4 
       5 -- Tequity's endpoint runs on the grading machine itself at loca
         -lhost:9000, and their load generator posts to localhost:8080 on
         - that same machine. Our API and worker run with host networking
         - (`network_mode: host`), so localhost inside our containers is 
         -the machine's own localhost, whatever address their endpoint li
         -stens on. This needs plain Docker Engine run as root, the norma
         -l install; rootless Docker and Docker Desktop keep host network
         -ing inside their own namespace or VM. The API listens on `:8080
         -`, every address, so both ::1 and 127.0.0.1 reach it.          
       6 -- Port 54329 on the grading machine is free. Postgres runs with
         - host networking on that port, listening on 127.0.0.1 only and 
         -reached at 127.0.0.1:54329, with its password in the compose fi
         -le, so it cannot clash with a Postgres already using the defaul
         -t port 5432 and cannot be reached from other machines.         
       7 -- Our test rig (a fake endpoint on 9000, a load generator and a
         - checker) runs as containers with host networking, never as pro
         -grams on the developer's machine. It has its own compose file, 
         -`rig/compose.yaml`, so the graders' `docker compose up` never s
         -tarts it. On Docker Desktop for Windows, host-network container
         -s and Windows cannot reach each other on localhost, while conta
         -iners inside Docker Desktop's Linux VM can, the same way they d
         -o on a Linux machine.                                          
       8 -- The relay and its test rig are written in Go. Every image is 
         -pinned to an exact patch tag (Postgres is `postgres:16.16-alpin
         -e`), the Go binary is built static with `CGO_ENABLED=0` in a mu
         -lti-stage build, and `go.sum` is committed.                    
       9 -- One worker process sends for every user, up to 200 users at a
         - time. When more users are ready than there are free slots, the
         - one whose oldest event has waited longest goes first. An event
         -'s `delivered_at` is the only record of progress: anything with
         -out it gets sent. After a 2xx, a failed database write of `deli
         -vered_at` is retried until it commits, and never causes a resen
         -d. The worker uses at most 20 database connections and the API 
         -at most 30, well under Postgres's 100.                         
      10 -- When healthy, Tequity's endpoint answers in about 10 ms or le
         -ss, with 500s on at most a few percent of requests. That is the
         - speed the heaviest user needs to clear the backlog of a 5-minu
         -te outage within 5 minutes of recovery, counting our own 5 ms p
         -er event and the waits before retries, if that user sends 30 of
         - the 100 events a second. The 30 is our guess, since the brief 
         -says only that a few users send far more than the rest; a user 
         -sending 50 a second would need about 4 ms. The test rig measure
         -s the endpoint's answer time. If the endpoint rate limits us be
         -low about 200 events a second during recovery, no design meets 
         -that.                                                          
       5 +- Tequity's endpoint runs on the grading machine itself at loca
         +lhost:9000, and their load generator posts to localhost:8080 on
         + that same machine.                                            
       6 +- The grading machine runs plain Docker Engine as root, the nor
         +mal install. Rootless Docker and Docker Desktop keep host netwo
         +rking inside their own namespace or VM, where our containers wo
         +uld not reach the machine's localhost.                         
       7 +- Ports 8080 and 54329 are free on the grading machine.        
       8 +- Tequity starts sending once `docker compose up` shows our ser
         +vices running.                                                 
       9 +- When healthy, the endpoint answers in about 10 ms or less, wi
         +th 500s on at most a few percent of requests, and during recove
         +ry it does not rate limit us below about 200 events a second. T
         +he design is sized for that with the heaviest user sending 30 o
         +f the 100 events a second; the 30 is our guess, since the brief
         + says only that a few users send far more than the rest. A user
         + sending 50 a second would need answers in about 4 ms. The test
         + rig measures the endpoint's answer time.                      
      10  - After the endpoint recovers, a heavy user's new events queue
          behind its backlog, in order, and can wait close to 5 minutes.
          We read the brief's 5-minute rule as covering every event held
          up by the outage, and its 30-second rule as applying again once
           that user's backlog is clear.
      12 -- A user's events wait while that user's oldest unsent event, b
         -y created_at, arrived less than 1 second ago. Arrival time is `
         -received_at`, set by Postgres's clock on insert; created_at is 
         -the load generator's clock and only decides order. A later even
         -t never goes out while an earlier one is still inside its hold.
         - An earlier event that arrives after a later one was already se
         -nt still goes out, late, and is logged.                        
      13 -- created_at has at most 6 decimal places of seconds. It is par
         -sed as RFC 3339 in Go, taken as UTC when it has no time zone, c
         -ut to microseconds (logged when digits are dropped) and stored 
         -as a Postgres timestamp. Two events of one user that tie are se
         -nt in arrival order.                                           
      14 -- "The same body" in the brief means the same bytes. Each body 
         -is stored as raw bytes and sent unchanged, with `Content-Type: 
         -application/json`.                                             
      15 -- Two posts with the same event_id are one event. event_id is t
         -he primary key and the insert skips duplicates, so both posts g
         -et a 200 and only one row exists.                              
      16 -- If an event_id arrives again with a different body, the first
         - body stands. The repeat gets a 200 and is logged.             
      17 -- Intake answers 200 only after Postgres has committed the even
         -t. It answers 400 when the body is not a JSON object, or when e
         -vent_id, user_id or created_at is missing, empty or unreadable,
         - and 413 when the body is over 1 MB. `type` and `data` pass thr
         -ough unchecked. Any database error gets a 503, which makes no p
         -romise, and every 400 is logged with its reason.               
      18 -- Any 2xx from the endpoint means saved. Any other status, a re
         -fused connection or a failed connect means not saved, and the e
         -vent is retried after 20 ms, doubling on each failure in a row 
         -up to 5 seconds. Redirects are not followed. An event the endpo
         -int keeps refusing blocks its user; each failure is logged at e
         -rror level with a count, and a status line every minute shows u
         -nsent events, the oldest one's age, the longest send in flight 
         -and how many users are blocked.                                
      19 -- A slow answer is always waited for, and an endpoint that neve
         -r answers is outside the brief's table. Sends have no overall t
         -imeout, only a 2-second connect timeout; a send in flight over 
         -60 seconds is logged as a warning with its event_id. TCP keepal
         -ive (the system checking an idle connection's other end is stil
         -l there) makes a connection whose other end has vanished fail. 
         -That failure, like any connection lost after the request went o
         -ut and before a status line came back, is the brief's "No conne
         -ction: We are down. Not saved.", and the event is sent again. T
         -he status line alone decides: a 200 means saved even if the res
         -t of the answer is cut off.                                    
      20 -- On a normal stop the worker starts no new sends and waits up 
         -to 120 seconds for the ones in progress, exiting as soon as the
         -y finish. The stop signal never cancels a send in progress or i
         -ts `delivered_at` write. A send still going after that, or one 
         -cut short by a hard kill, is sent again on restart and may be s
         -aved twice.                                                    
      21 -- A 429 pauses only the user whose request got it, since the br
         -ief does not say whether the limit is per user or for the whole
         - endpoint, and a per-user pause works under both. The pause las
         -ts its Retry-After seconds, and at least 1 second, which also c
         -overs a missing header, a value that is not a whole number and 
         -`Retry-After: 0`. A 429 does not raise the retry wait.         
      22 -- Tequity starts sending once `docker compose up` shows our ser
         -vices running. Posts in the first few seconds may be refused, a
         -nd a refused post carries no 2xx promise. An event we refused (
         -during startup, or with a 503 during a Postgres restart) that T
         -equity resends after that user's later events went out arrives 
         -out of order; our promise for it starts only with our 2xx. Post
         -gres keeps its data in a named volume, so stored events survive
         - `docker compose down` and `up`. Only `down -v` deletes them, a
         -nd a schema change needs it, since the init SQL runs only on an
         - empty data folder. The API and worker never exit on a database
         - error; they log it and retry, and a panic in one user's loop i
         -s caught and logged without stopping the others.               
      11 +- A user's events reach us out of created_at order by at most a
         +bout 1 second. An earlier event that comes later than that, aft
         +er the user's later events went out, is still sent, late and ou
         +t of order.                                                    
      12 +- created_at has at most 6 decimal places of seconds, and one w
         +ithout a time zone is in UTC.                                  
      13 +- "The same body" in the brief means the same bytes.           
      14 +- A post that repeats an event_id is a resend of the same event
         +, even if its body differs, and the first body stands.         
      15 +- A valid event is a JSON object under 1 MB with a non-empty ev
         +ent_id, user_id and created_at. Anything else gets a 400.      
      16 +- Our promise starts with our 2xx. A post we refused, during st
         +artup or with a 503 while Postgres restarts, carries none; if T
         +equity resends it after that user's later events went out, it a
         +rrives out of order.                                           
      17 +- Any 2xx from the endpoint means saved, and any other status m
         +eans not saved.                                                
      18 +- A slow answer always comes in the end. The brief's table has 
         +no endpoint that never answers.                                
      19 +- A connection lost after our request went out, before a status
         + line came back, is the brief's "No connection: We are down. No
         +t saved." A 200 status line means saved even if the rest of the
         + answer is cut off.                                            
      20 +- Retry-After is a whole number of seconds. A missing or zero v
         +alue, or one that is not a whole number, counts as 1 second.   
      21 +- The brief does not say whether a 429's limit is per user or c
         +overs the whole endpoint, so the design has to work under both.

         -nt in Postgres before it answers, and a worker passes the store
         -d events on to Tequity's endpoint on port 9000, once each and i
         -n `created_at` order per user. docs/problem.md states the probl
         -em in full, and docs/decisions.md gives the reason for every ch
         -oice.                                                          
      25 +Tequity's take-home. An intake API on port 8080 stores each eve

         +tions about Tequity's side. docs/problem.md states the problem

         +hile it waits on port 9000. pgbench with 20 connections ran abo
         +ut 3,460 worker steps a second (mark delivered, then look up th
         +e next event), 17 times the 200 a second the outage maths needs
         +. Go's HTTP client keeps only 2 idle connections per host by de
         +fault, so most of 200 sends at once would open a new TCP connec
         +tion each time; the worker sets `MaxIdleConnsPerHost` to 200. (
         +Added 2026-11-19 after review.)                                
      90 
      91  The worker keeps in memory which users are in flight, plus any
          pause or retry wait. None of that has to survive a restart. Aft
          er one, anything without `delivered_at` is sent again, and the
          worst a forgotten wait costs is one extra 500 or 429.

      178 
      179  created_at is parsed and stored as a Postgres `timestamptz`, w
           hich keeps microseconds. The brief's example has 3 decimal pla
           ces, and the README assumes at most 6. If finer digits ever ro
           und two events of one user to the same value, they go out in t
           he order they reached us. There is no unique rule on (user_id,
            created_at), so a tie can never cause an event to be refused.
      180 
      181 -Intake parses created_at in Go, never in Postgres. It uses Go'
          -s RFC 3339 layout with fractional seconds (`time.RFC3339Nano`)
          -, which accepts `Z` and offsets such as `+05:30`. If the strin
          -g has no time zone, it is taken as UTC. Postgres would take it
          - in the session's zone, which depends on settings. The parsed 
          -time is cut to microseconds by our code, with a log line whene
          -ver digits are dropped, and passed to Postgres as a `time.Time
          -`. Without the cut, Postgres would round extra digits while Go
          -'s driver cuts them, and two code paths could disagree on orde
          -r. A string that matches neither layout gets a 400.           
      181 +created_at stays a string in JavaScript and never becomes a Ja
          +vaScript `Date`, which keeps only milliseconds and would merge
          + events that differ in the 4th to 6th decimal. Intake checks t
          +he string against an RFC 3339 pattern, which allows `Z`, offse
          +ts such as `+05:30`, or no zone, cuts any fraction past 6 deci
          +mals out of the string, with a log line, and passes it to Post
          +gres as text cast with `$1::timestamptz`. Postgres runs with `
          +-c timezone=UTC`, so a string with no zone is taken as UTC wha
          +tever the session. Ordering stays in SQL; created_at is never 
          +read back into JavaScript to compare. A string that fails the 
          +pattern gets a 400, and so does one the cast refuses (a month 
          +13, say): Postgres's data-exception errors (SQLSTATE class 22)
          + map to 400, every other database error to 503.               
      182 
      183  The review proposed refusing a created_at with no time zone. W
           e take it as UTC. Order only matters within one user, and any
           fixed rule for zoneless times keeps that order, while a 400 dr
           ops the event. (Added 2026-11-19 after review.)

      227 +Sends use Node's `http.request`, never `fetch`: `fetch` follow
          +s redirects by default and has a hidden 300-second limit on he
          +aders and body. `http.request` has no time limit unless one is
          + set, and its `timeout` option stays unused, since it fires on
          + any quiet socket, a slow answer included. The connect step ha
          +s a 2-second timeout, made with a timer that is cleared on the
          + socket's `connect` event; a reused keep-alive connection skip
          +s it. A refused or failed connect means the request was never 
          +sent, so the event is not saved and is retried. TCP keepalive

          + a backstop. Keepalive catches a connection whose other end ha
          +s vanished without closing it. An endpoint that is alive but s
          +low still answers the probes, so its request keeps waiting.

      229  On localhost the endpoint's own kernel answers keepalive probe
           s, so an endpoint that holds a connection open and never answe
           rs keeps that send, and its user, waiting for good. We accept
           that. A ceiling followed by a resend is the timeout-then-resen
           d this entry rules out, and the brief's table has no endpoint
           that never answers: a slow answer "is still coming". Only that
            one user waits; the other send slots carry on. To make such a
            send visible, the worker logs a warning with the event_id and
            user_id for any send in flight over 60 seconds, and again eac
           h further minute. (Added 2026-11-19 after review, which propos

      242 
      243  A short grace period is a timeout-then-resend in another form:
            a slow send killed at the deadline is resent on restart, whic
           h is the double save the no-timeout entry rules out. With 120
           seconds, only a send slower than two minutes is cut off. The w
           orker exits as soon as its sends finish, so the full 120 secon
           ds is spent only when a send is that slow, and then a slow sto
           p is the price of avoiding a double save.

      245 -Two things in the Go code and image make this work. The HTTP c
          -all and the `delivered_at` write run on a context that SIGTERM
          - does not cancel (`context.WithoutCancel`), so the stop signal
          - cannot abort the send we are waiting for or the write after i
          -t. And the image starts the binary with an exec-form `ENTRYPOI
          -NT ["/relay", ...]`, so SIGTERM reaches Go at all; with the sh
          -ell form, `/bin/sh` is process 1 and does not pass the signal 
          -on.                                                           
      245 +Two things in the Node code and image make this work. The work
          +er has a SIGTERM handler that stops the poll and the start of 
          +new sends, and lets every send in flight and its `delivered_at
          +` write finish before it closes the Pool and exits; nothing in
          + it aborts a request. And each service starts Node with an exe
          +c-form `CMD ["node", "worker.js"]` (`api.js` for the API), so 
          +Node is process 1 and gets the signal. Node as process 1 ignor
          +es SIGTERM unless it has a handler, so without one every stop 
          +would end in a kill. The API's handler stops taking new posts 
          +and lets the inserts in progress commit.                      
      246 
      247  The review also proposed stamping `attempt_started_at` on each
            event before sending it, so that a restart could log every ev

           1.2 ms per event: BEGIN, SET LOCAL synchronous_commit = off, U
           PDATE and COMMIT. That would raise our time per event from abo
           ut 5 ms to 6 ms and lower the endpoint speed the heaviest user
            needs from about 10.7 ms to about 9.7 ms (see the 10 ms entry
           ), all for a log line after a crash. At startup the worker log

      265  Postgres keeps its data in a named volume, `pgdata:/var/lib/po

            we answered 2xx for is gone. Only `docker compose down -v` de

           a folder, so a change to the schema needs `down -v` to take ef

          -ing they log each database error and retry inside the process:

          - otherwise kill the whole process, and the restart would hit t

● I've made the four commits. Nothing was amended or reset, and the chat export
  is still unstaged.

  - ddc6bcd: each event's delivered_at is committed before the user's next send,
    never alongside it. The reason in the entry is yours: a crash between the
    two would resend event 1 after event 2.
  - d63b22b: the README now lists only assumptions about Tequity's side, and the
    CLAUDE.md rule says the same. "No ceiling on slow sends" needed no commit,
    since the docs already said it.
  - 589fddc: Node.js 24 replaces Go, with pg as the only dependency. Every Go
    detail is rewritten for Node. Four Node facts changed the docs beyond a
    straight swap:
    - http.request's timeout option stays unused. It would also fire while a
      slow answer is still coming, which would undo the no-ceiling decision. The
      2-second connect limit is a timer instead.
    - Postgres now starts with timezone=UTC. created_at now stays a string and
      is converted inside Postgres, so this is what keeps "no time zone means
      UTC" true.
    - A malformed date such as month 13 gets a 400, not a 503. Postgres now
      rejects it during that conversion, and a 503 would wrongly signal our own
      fault.
    - The keepalive probe interval and count are Linux's defaults. Node can't
      set them, and host networking blocks per-container settings. On localhost
      it doesn't matter much, since a dead endpoint closes its connections at
      once.
  - f680d19: decisions.md went from about 6,500 words to 3,200. That's 25
    entries, the longest 149 words, with no notes on earlier versions. CLAUDE.md
    now sets the 150-word limit and drops its rule asking for a dated note on
    each change.

  I didn't adopt one part of the review's item 11: it says to refuse a
  created_at with no time zone. I kept the earlier decision to treat it as UTC.
  Say if you want that reversed.

  Next step is the test rig.

› Message from ecc:agent-evaluator
  Review of the Tequity take-home docs and git history, read against the brief
  (PDF text taken from pdftotext). Items are ordered by importance.

  **D1. DOC-FIX. README.md lines 9-21: design choices sit inside the assumptions
  list.**
  - The line 9 "The design is sized for that..." and "The test rig measures the
  endpoint's answer time" are design and test statements.
  - The line 5-6 explanation of why rootless Docker breaks our containers is
  design reasoning.
  - Line 7 "54329" is our own design port, not Tequity's.
  - Line 10 "queue behind its backlog, in order" and line 11 "is still sent,
  late and out of order" are what we do.
  - Line 14 "the first body stands", line 15 "Anything else gets a 400" and line
  20 "counts as 1 second" are our rules.
  - Line 16 "a 503 while Postgres restarts" and line 21 "so the design has to
  work under both" are design.
  - Fix: state each as a bare belief about Tequity ("Retry-After is a whole
  number") and move the response to decisions.md.

  **D2. DOC-FIX. README.md line 15 vs decisions.md "Intake answers 200 only
  after the commit".**
  - README says a valid event is under 1 MB and "Anything else gets a 400".
  - decisions.md gives 413 for an oversize body, 503 for database errors, and
  400 for an unparseable created_at.
  - README's validity definition never mentions created_at being parseable.
  - Fix: delete the response codes from the README line, or make them match.

  **D3. DOC-FIX. README.md line 8 vs line 16.**
  - Line 8 says Tequity sends only after `docker compose up` shows our services
  running.
  - Line 16 then covers posts refused during startup.
  - problem.md's last open point says posts may arrive before Postgres or the
  API is ready.
  - Fix: pick one. For example, "Tequity may start posting at any moment after
  `up` begins".

  **D4. DOC-FIX. docs/problem.md says "It holds no solutions" (line 3), but it
  contains design.**
  - "What must always hold" says "A user's event goes out only after all their
  earlier events are saved". The brief does not require this.
  - The open points say "One request in flight per user caps a heavy user", "if
  we wait for stragglers" and "through the per-user hold". These name the later
  design.
  - Fix: reword these as the brief's risk (order vs speed), or drop the claim of
  "no solutions".

  **D5. DOC-FIX. decisions.md, "sized for an endpoint that answers in about 10
  ms".**
  - The formula T = (a + o) ÷ (1 − p) + p × 20 ms is never derived in words.
  - "18,000 events" is not derived. It is 9,000 backlog plus about 8,850 new
  arrivals over 295 s.
  - "20 ms the first retry wait" and "Noticing recovery can take 5 seconds" are
  defined only in a later entry.
  - The 200 a second figure in the one-worker entry has no derivation. It is 100
  × (300+300)/300, which was in commit a9aa31f and is not in the repo files.
  - o = 5 ms comes from pgbench on a Windows laptop's Docker Desktop VM. That is
  not the graders' machine, and the entry does not say so.
  - Fix: add a two-line worked example, and say the o figure is laptop-measured.

  **D6. DOC-FIX. decisions.md, pool entry and one-worker entry: pgbench evidence
  cannot be reproduced.**
  - `-f step.sql` names a file that is not in the repo, and "worker step" is
  undefined.
  - Fix: commit the SQL file under rig/ or describe the step in one sentence.

  **D7. DOC-FIX. decisions.md, "Connection pools are sized by hand".**
  - "Dropped: one large pool for both processes" is incoherent. The API and the
  worker are separate processes and cannot share a pool.
  - Fix: replace it with a real alternative, such as the `pg` default of 10
  each, or delete the line.

  **D8. DOC-FIX. decisions.md, poll entry: the evidence does not match the
  load.**
  - The 1.758 ms figure is for 1,000 unsent rows. The design's stress case is
  30,000 or more.
  - The commit estimated 25-30 ms at 30,000 but decisions.md omits it.
  - The poll query is `DISTINCT ON` over all unsent rows every 200 ms.
  - "Dropped: filtering on received_at before the pick, which skips a held
  oldest event" is unclear. It means a later event goes out first.
  - Fix: state the 30,000-row figure and give a three-event example.

  **D9. DOC-FIX. decisions.md, "The relay is written in Node.js 24".**
  - "the owner", "Dropped: Go, picked earlier without asking the owner": "owner"
  is undefined to a grader, and this is process history rather than technical
  reasoning.
  - "100 events a second is light work for Node" is unmeasured.
  - Fix: say "the author" and give the technical reason, or state plainly that
  it is a preference.

  **D10. DOC-FIX. decisions.md, "Every build input is pinned" and "Every text
  file has LF".**
  - "Node gets an exact tag when the Dockerfile is written" is a promise, not a
  decision.
  - "The repo has no shell scripts beyond..." is a claim about files that do not
  exist yet.
  - Fix: mark these as pending until the code exists, or drop the future-tense
  lines.

  **D11. DOC-FIX. decisions.md, "A send has no overall time limit".**
  - It names `setKeepAlive(true, 10000)` as a backstop. Commit b5dfece says on
  localhost the endpoint's kernel answers the probes, so it is useless here.
  - The entry omits this.
  - "a hidden 300-second limit" and "Linux's own probe interval and count" are
  unexplained.
  - Fix: state that the keepalive cannot help on localhost.

  **D12. DOC-FIX. decisions.md last entry ("Before handover, a fresh clone runs
  on Linux").**
  - This is a plan, not a design decision.
  - It promises that "the README records its commands and output", and the

  - "which is decided then" is vague.
  - Fix: move it to a TODO list, or turn it into a decision once the run has
  happened.

  **D13. DOC-FIX. decisions.md entry order and dates.**

  - The "Bodies are stored as raw bytes" entry is dated 11-18 and sits among

  - The dates no longer mean anything now that entries are rewritten in place.

  - Examples: "Dropped: relying on each machine's git settings." "Dropped: 20
  seconds." (bare, in the stop entry). "Dropped: a migration tool, which one SQL
  file does not need." "Dropped: handing over after testing on Docker Desktop
  alone."
  - Fix: keep "Dropped" only where there was a real option and say why it lost,
  and remove the slot elsewhere.

  **G2. GENERATED. problem.md open points use one template.**
  - 13 of them end "This can break X." or "This can break exactly once." in the
  same cadence.
  - "Port 9000 can answer outside the table: a 400, 404, 503, a redirect, a 429
  without a usable Retry-After, or a cut connection after sending" is a list of
  cases without weighing which are likely.
  - Fix: cut the open points to those the brief leaves truly open, and vary the
  shape.

  **G3. GENERATED. Claims without evidence.**
  - "100 events a second is light work for Node"
  - "Docker Desktop ... never runs a firewall"
  - "Node as process 1 ignores SIGTERM without a handler"
  - "which on localhost cost under 1 ms"
  - "fsync and synchronous_commit on" is the default and reads as padding.
  - "Listening on 127.0.0.1 keeps other machines out."
  - Fix: cut them or attach a command.

  **G4. GENERATED. Over-precise figures.**
  - "tps = 3460.405120" and "1.758 ms" are shown as proof, but they come from a
  laptop VM and say nothing about the graders' machine.
  - Fix: round them and say where they were measured.

  **G5. GENERATED. Stock phrasing.**
  - "Failures are logged loudly", "so neither can break exactly once", and the
  repeated ", since ..." and ", which ..." tails.
  - Fix: rewrite a few in plain direct sentences.

  **G6. GENERATED. README line 9 is one very long, dense sentence set.**
  - It packs 5 numbers and 3 conditions into one bullet.
  - Fix: split it into separate assumptions: latency, error rate, rate limiting,
  heavy-user share.

● Agent "Review docs as the grader" finished · 2m 2s

› Message from ecc:architect
  I read all three pages of the brief, docs/problem.md, docs/decisions.md and
  README.md. No code exists, so every item below concerns the design as written.
  One decision fails a pass condition as written, and several others leave
  risks.

  **1. FAILS: "A send has no overall time limit" (the 2 s connect timer)**
  - **What goes wrong:** The timer is "cleared on the socket's `connect` event".
  A keep-alive socket that is reused is already connected, so `connect` never
  fires on it. Sequence: send on a reused socket, the endpoint answers slowly
  (over 2 s), the timer destroys the request, the design treats it as "lost
  before status line" and resends. The endpoint saves the event twice. With 100
  events a second most sends reuse a socket, so this happens on every slow
  answer.
  - **Sure:** certain. Node emits `connect` only for a new connection.
  - **Fix:** Start the timer only when `socket.connecting` is true (or
  `!req.reusedSocket`). The simpler option is to drop the timer: on localhost a
  connection is refused or accepted at once.

  **2. RISK/GAP: "The poll picks ready users fairly" and "One worker process"**
  - **What goes wrong:** If polling runs on `setInterval(200)`, one poll can
  still be waiting while the next starts, for example when the pool queue is
  behind 200 loops or the poll query is slow. Poll P1 sends `$1` without user A
  and A has not started yet. P2 does the same. Both return A, so two loops run
  for A. Both pick A's oldest unsent event, and the endpoint saves it twice,
  possibly out of order.
  - **Also:** The poll query was timed with 1,000 unsent rows, but after a
  5-minute outage it scans 30,000 every 200 ms. `Heap Fetches: 0` only holds
  while the table is freshly vacuumed.
  - **Sure:** likely. The documents do not say how polls are scheduled.
  - **Fix:** Schedule the next poll only after the previous one finishes. Before
  starting a loop, add the user to the in-memory in-flight map in the same
  synchronous step and skip any user already in it. Time the query again with
  30k rows after updates have churned the table.

  **3. RISK: "A 429 pauses only the user who got it"**
  - **What goes wrong:** Suppose the limit covers the whole endpoint and sits
  near our rate, and we have up to 200 sends in flight. The heavy user sends the
  most, so it gets most of the 429s, and each one costs it at least 1 s. If 10%
  of its sends get a 429, it manages about 10 a second against 30 arriving. Its
  backlog grows without end and the timing condition fails. Light users soak up
  the limit meanwhile.
  - **Sure:** unsure. It depends on how Tequity's limit works.
  - **Fix:** Keep the per-user pause, and on any 429 also halve the number of
  slots in use (minimum 1), growing it back by one per 200.

  **4. GAP: "A database error or a failed loop never stops a process" and "The
  status line decides"**
  - **What goes wrong:** The try/catch covers only the user loops.
    - A `ClientRequest` 'error' with no listener throws and kills the worker.
  This includes an error that comes after the promise has settled, because that
  listener was removed.
    - So do a rejection in the poll loop and one in the SIGTERM path.
    - The restart then resends every send that was in flight, and the endpoint
  may save some twice.
    - Separately, `res.resume()` is specified only for a 200. A 500 or 429
  response that is never drained keeps its socket out of the pool, and sockets
  leak.
  - **Sure:** likely, if the code follows the document literally.
  - **Fix:** Attach permanent no-op 'error' listeners to `req` and `res`, call
  `res.resume()` on every status, and wrap the poll loop and shutdown in
  try/catch.

  **5. RISK: "The design is sized for an endpoint that answers in about 10 ms"
  (o = 5 ms)**
  - **What goes wrong:** o was measured on the build laptop. `delivered_at` is
  committed with fsync before each next send, so a heavy user's speed depends on
  how fast the grading machine's disk flushes. A cloud VM disk taking 5 to 10
  ms per fsync gives o of about 10 ms. Then a must be at most about 6 ms, or the
  30-a-second user misses 5 minutes after recovery.
  - **Sure:** unsure. It depends on the graders' hardware.
  - **Fix:** Use `SET synchronous_commit = off` on the worker's connections
  only. The cost is that a Postgres or OS crash could lose the last ~0.6 s of
  `delivered_at` writes, which would be sent again. Tequity never touches our
  containers.

  **6. RISK: "Intake answers 200 only after the commit" (Node server defaults)**
  - **What goes wrong:** Node's `server.keepAliveTimeout` is 5 s. If the load
  generator reuses a connection that has been idle for about 5 s, our server may
  close it at the same moment. The client then sees "socket hang up" on a POST
  that we never received. Tequity may count that as intake not accepting.
  - **Sure:** unsure. It depends on Tequity's client.
  - **Fix:** Set `keepAliveTimeout` to 65 s and `headersTimeout` just above it.

  **7. OVERBUILT: "A send has no overall time limit" and "Connection pools are
  sized by hand"**
  - **Cut `socket.setKeepAlive(true, 10000)`:** An Agent with `keepAlive: true`
  already turns on TCP keepalive (with `keepAliveMsecs` at 1000 ms). On
  localhost a dead peer answers with a reset anyway.
  - **Cut `maxFreeSockets: 200`:** Node's default is 256.
  - **Sure:** certain.
  - **Fix:** Keep only `new http.Agent({ keepAlive: true })`.

  **8. OVERBUILT: "Failures are logged loudly, with a status line every
  minute"**
  - **What to cut:** The per-minute status line, the 60-second warnings for
  sends in flight and the count of stuck users protect no pass condition.
  - **Sure:** certain.
  - **Fix:** Keep one error log line per failed attempt and the unsent count at
  startup.

  **9. GAP (no effect on any pass condition): README timing assumption**
  - **What goes wrong:** The outage maths counts only the 18,000-event backlog.
  New events that arrive during catch-up wait behind it for up to about 295 s.
  The README's reading covers this, and no design that keeps order could do
  better.
  - **Sure:** certain.
  - **Fix:** None needed. Keep that README line prominent, because a grader
  reading the 30-second rule strictly will flag it.

  **Claims checked and correct**
  - Node as PID 1 ignores SIGTERM unless the process sets a handler. Exec-form
  `CMD` is the right choice.
  - `pg` Pool emits 'error' for an idle client, and with no listener that
  crashes Node.
  - `pg` Pool defaults to `max` 10.
  - `pg` sends a Buffer parameter in binary, and reads bytea back as a Buffer.
  - `pool.query` runs one statement and commits it on its own, so the design
  never needs a transaction.
  - `fetch` follows redirects and has 300-second header and body timeouts by
  default.
  - The `http` request `timeout` option is an inactivity timer. It fires during
  a slow answer but does nothing unless a listener destroys the request.
  - Node's `http` module never resends.
  - `autoSelectFamily` is on by default in Node 24.
  - A JS `Date` keeps only milliseconds.
  - When a reused keep-alive socket was already closed by the server, the error
  comes before any status line, so resending is correct.
  - `req.end(buffer)` sets Content-Length.
  - `ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING` makes the second of two simultaneous inserts wait
  for the first to commit.
  - The postgres image's temporary server during init listens on no TCP port, so
  the health check cannot pass early.

  **Sound, left out:** host networking, Postgres on 54329, keeping `created_at`
  a string cast in SQL, raw bytea bodies, `delivered_at` committed before the
  next send, the 1 s per-user hold, 120 s for a normal stop, the named volume,
  startup ordering.

  Not checked: nothing could be run, because no code exists. Every point about
  Node and `pg` behaviour comes from knowledge of their source, not from tests.
  Item 1 needs fixing before the worker is written; items 2 and 4 depend on how
  the loop is coded.

● Both reviews are in. Below is one merged list, with each item checked against
  the docs. For every item I give my call: do it, do it differently, push back,
  or skip. Nothing is changed yet.

  Fails

  - 1. The connect timer kills slow answers on reused connections (architect)
    - The entry says the 2-second timer is "cleared on the socket's connect
      event". A reused keep-alive socket never fires connect, so a slow answer
      on one gets destroyed after 2 s and resent. That's a double save.
    - This one is my fault. The Node commit said "a reused keep-alive connection
      skips it", and I lost that clause when I cut the entry to 150 words.
    - My call: drop the timer. On localhost a connect is accepted or refused at
      once. Whether a send counts as "not sent" or "unknown" is then decided by
      whether the socket ever connected.

  Risks

  - 2. Two polls can start two loops for one user (architect)
    - If polls run on setInterval, a slow poll overlaps the next. Both can
      return user A, so two loops send A's oldest event twice, possibly out of
      order.
    - My call: do it. Start each poll only after the last one finishes, and
      check the in-memory in-flight set at the moment a loop starts, not only
      through $1.
  - 3. A stray error can still crash the worker (architect)
    - An error on a request with no listener, a rejection in the poll loop, or
      one in the SIGTERM path each kill the process. On restart, every send in
      flight goes out again.
    - Also, only a 200 is said to be drained, and an undrained 500 or 429 holds
      its socket.
    - My call: do it. Keep a permanent error listener on req and res, call
      res.resume() on every status, and wrap the poll loop and shutdown in
      try/catch.
  - 4. Node's server closes idle connections after 5 seconds (architect)
    - A load-generator connection reused right at that moment gets "socket hang
      up" on a post we never received. That can count against "intake stays up".
    - My call: do it. Set keepAliveTimeout to 65 s, with headersTimeout just
      above it.
  - 5. The graders' disk may make our 5 ms per event slower (architect)
    - The reviewer suggests synchronous_commit = off on the worker's
      connections.
    - My call: push back. A Postgres crash would then lose the last delivered_at
      writes and resend several events in a row. That's the double save plus
      order break you rejected for parallel commits. I'd keep durable commits
      and measure the time per event on the Linux run.
  - 6. A 429 limit for the whole endpoint can starve the heavy user (architect)
    - If 10% of its sends get a 429 with a 1-second pause each, it manages about
      10 a second.
    - The reviewer suggests also halving the number of users in flight on
      each 429.
    - My call: keep per-user pauses, which you chose on purpose. Make the README
      assumption explicit instead: the endpoint rarely rate limits when
      healthy. Under heavy rate limiting no design keeps the heavy user's order
      and timing.
  - 7. The poll query is unmeasured at 30,000 unsent rows (both reviewers)
    - The 1.758 ms was at 1,000 rows, and "Heap Fetches: 0" holds only right
      after a vacuum.
    - My call: measure it in the test rig, after updates have churned the table.
      Until then, say in the entry what was measured and that 30,000 is
      untested.

  Over built

  - 8. Cut socket.setKeepAlive(true, 10000) and maxFreeSockets: 200 (architect,
    evaluator)
    - An Agent with keepAlive: true already turns TCP keepalive on, and Node's
      default for maxFreeSockets is already 256. Keepalive can't help on
      localhost anyway.
    - My call: cut both and keep new http.Agent({ keepAlive: true }).
  - 9. The status line and stuck-user count protect no pass condition
    (architect)
    - My call: cut the minute status line and the stuck-user count. Keep the
      error log per failed attempt and the 60-second warning, which is what

  - 10. "Dropped:" appears on every entry, even with no real option (evaluator)

       1 +# Review of the committed decisions, 18 Nov 2026               
       2 +                                                               
       3 +Five reviewers checked docs/decisions.md against docs/problem.m
         +d and the brief, each in its own area: worker design, Postgres,
         + silent failures, Docker and compose, and a grader trying to br
         +eak the four pass conditions. Nothing was run. Each item below 
         +names the decision it changes, what goes wrong, and the fix. Wh
         +ere reviewers overlapped, the items are merged. Items marked "n
         +ew" are not covered by any decision yet.                       
       4 +                                                               
       5 +## Language change, do this first                              
       6 +                                                               
       7 +0. Node.js replaces Go (decision: write the relay in Go and run
         + it on Postgres 16). The owner wants Node.js; the brief allows 
         +any language. Fix: a new decision commit that edits that entry:
         + Node.js 24 LTS with the exact patch version pinned, plain Java
         +Script with no build step, Node's own `http` module for the API
         + server and for calls to 9000, and `pg` as the only dependency.
         + The API and the worker stay two separate processes (same image
         +, two compose services), so a busy worker never slows intake. T
         +he test rig is written in Node too. Several fixes below name No
         +de details for this reason.                                    
       8 +                                                               
       9 +## Fails a pass condition as decided                           
      10 +                                                               
      11 +1. Postgres data volume (decision: start once Postgres is healt
         +hy; Postgres on 127.0.0.1:54329). No decision names a data volu
         +me, so the image makes an anonymous one. After `docker compose 
         +down` and `up`, every unsent event we answered 2xx for is gone.
         + Fix: named volume `pgdata:/var/lib/postgresql/data`, keep fsyn
         +c and synchronous_commit at their defaults. README note: a sche
         +ma change needs `docker compose down -v`, since the init SQL ru
         +ns only on an empty data folder.                               
      12 +                                                               
      13 +2. Partial answer (decision: no overall timeout, resend only wh
         +en the connection is lost after sending). A 200 status line fol
         +lowed by a cut body counts as unknown and gets resent, which sa
         +ves the event twice. Fix: decide saved or not on the status lin
         +e alone (the `response` event of Node's `http.request`), then d
         +rain the body with `res.resume()` and ignore any error from it.
         + Use `http.request`, not `fetch`: `fetch` follows redirects by 
         +default and has a hidden 300 second limit on headers and body. 
      14 +                                                               
      15 +3. Retry waits break the timing maths (decisions: any 2xx is sa
         +ved, retry from 0.5 up to 5 seconds; the 14 ms assumption). The
         + maths left out retry waits. With 2% 500s and a 14 ms endpoint,
         + each event of the heavy user costs about 24 ms, so about 41 a 
         +second, and it clears about 12,500 of the 18,000 events it owes
         + in 5 minutes. Fix: retry a 500 at once or after about 20 ms; k
         +eep the 0.5 to 5 second backoff only for refused connections an
         +d repeated failures. Redo the maths with retry waits, the extra
         + query per event, and up to 5 seconds before recovery is notice
         +d.                                                             
      16 +                                                               
      17 +4. The hold has no arrival time (decision: hold a user while it
         +s oldest unsent event is under 1 second old). The table has an 
         +arrival number but no arrival time, and holding on created_at u
         +ses the load generator's clock. Fix: `received_at timestamptz N
         +OT NULL DEFAULT now()`, from Postgres's clock, plus `seq bigint
         + GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY` for ties. The hold must be appli
         +ed after picking each user's oldest event, never in a WHERE bef
         +ore it, or the user's second event gets picked first:          
      18 +                                                               
      19 +```sql                                                         
      20 +SELECT user_id FROM (                                          
      21 +  SELECT DISTINCT ON (user_id) user_id, received_at            
      22 +  FROM events WHERE delivered_at IS NULL                       
      23 +  ORDER BY user_id, created_at, seq) o                         
      24 +WHERE received_at < now() - interval '1 second' AND user_id <> 
         +ALL($1);                                                       
      25 +```                                                            
      26 +                                                               
      27 +## Risks                                                       
      28 +                                                               
      29 +5. Database error after a 200 (decisions: one worker process, d
         +elivered_at the only record; any 2xx is saved). If the `UPDATE 
         +... SET delivered_at` fails after the endpoint said 200, a loop
         + that treats it as a failed send resends and saves twice. Fix: 
         +after a 2xx, retry only that UPDATE until it commits. Never res
         +end because of a database error.                               
      30 +                                                               
      31 +6. Endpoint that holds the connection and never answers (decisi
         +on: no overall timeout, TCP keepalive). On localhost the endpoi
         +nt's own kernel answers keepalive probes, so keepalive never en
         +ds this case and the user is stuck for good. Fix: a ceiling of 
         +about 120 seconds (a timer that calls `req.destroy()`), after w
         +hich the outcome counts as unknown and the event is resent. Log
         + a warning with the event_id for any send in flight over 60 sec
         +onds.                                                          
      32 +                                                               
      33 +7. The 20-second stop (decision: in-progress sends get 20 secon
         +ds). It brings back the timeout-then-resend that decision 6's e
         +ntry removed, and a shutdown that aborts requests can cut a sen
         +d or the delivered_at write. Node running as PID 1 ignores SIGT
         +ERM unless it has a handler, so without one every stop ends in 
         +a kill. Fix: `stop_grace_period: 120s`; exec-form `CMD ["node",
         + "worker.js"]`; a SIGTERM handler that stops starting new sends
         + and lets the ones in flight and their delivered_at writes fini
         +sh; stamp `attempt_started_at` before each send and, at startup
         +, log every row with an attempt but no delivered_at as "outcome
         + unknown, resending".                                          
      34 +                                                               
      35 +8. Connection pools (decision: one worker process). Postgres al
         +lows 100 connections, shared by the API and the worker. `pg`'s 
         +Pool defaults to 10, which is too few for 200 users sending, an
         +d too many pools would break intake. Without keep-alive, every 
         +send opens a new TCP connection. Fix: worker pool about 20, API
         + pool about 30, never hold a database connection while waiting 
         +on port 9000; an `http.Agent` with `keepAlive: true` for calls 
         +to 9000.                                                       
      36 +                                                               
      37 +9. 429 details (decision: a 429 pauses all sending). `Retry-Aft
         +er: 0` or a missing value gives a tight loop. Nothing says whet
         +her a 429 also raises a user's backoff. Fix: pause at least 1 s
         +econd; a 429 does not raise the per-user backoff. Item 20 chang
         +es who gets paused.                                            
      38 +                                                               
      39 +10. Index for the 200 ms query (new). Without it the query scan
         +s a table that grows with every delivered row. Fix: `CREATE IND
         +EX events_pending ON events (user_id, created_at, seq) INCLUDE 
         +(received_at) WHERE delivered_at IS NULL;`

       3  ## Assumptions
       4 
       5  - Tequity's endpoint runs on the grading machine itself at loca
          lhost:9000, and their load generator posts to localhost:8080 on
           that same machine.
       6 -- The grading machine runs plain Docker Engine as root, the nor
         -mal install. Rootless Docker and Docker Desktop keep host netwo
         -rking inside their own namespace or VM, where our containers wo
         -uld not reach the machine's localhost.                         
       6 +- The grading machine runs plain Docker Engine as root, the nor
         +mal install, not rootless Docker or Docker Desktop.            
       7  - Ports 8080 and 54329 are free on the grading machine.
       8 -- Tequity starts sending once `docker compose up` shows our ser
         -vices running.                                                 
       9 -- When healthy, the endpoint answers in about 10 ms or less, wi
         -th 500s on at most a few percent of requests, and during recove
         -ry it does not rate limit us below about 200 events a second. T
         -he design is sized for that with the heaviest user sending 30 o
         -f the 100 events a second; the 30 is our guess, since the brief
         - says only that a few users send far more than the rest. A user
         - sending 50 a second would need answers in about 4 ms. The test
         - rig measures the endpoint's answer time.                      
      10 -- When healthy, the endpoint rarely answers 429.               
      11 -- After the endpoint recovers, a heavy user's new events queue 
         -behind its backlog, in order, and can wait close to 5 minutes. 
         -We read the brief's 5-minute rule as covering every event held 
         -up by the outage, and its 30-second rule as applying again once
         - that user's backlog is clear.                                 
      12 -- A user's events reach us out of created_at order by at most a
         -bout 1 second. An earlier event that comes later than that, aft
         -er the user's later events went out, is still sent, late and ou
         -t of order.                                                    
       8 +- Tequity may post as soon as `docker compose up` starts. A pos
         +t refused before we are ready carries no promise, and if Tequit
         +y resends it after that user's later events went out, it may ar
         +rive out of order.                                             
       9 +- When healthy, the endpoint answers in about 10 ms or less.   
      10 +- When healthy, at most a few percent of the endpoint's answers
         + are 500s, and 429s are rare.                                  
      11 +- During recovery, the endpoint does not rate limit us below ab
         +out 200 events a second.                                       
      12 +- The heaviest user sends about 30 of the 100 events a second. 
         +The brief says only that a few users send far more than the res
         +t.                                                             
      13 +- The brief's 5-minute rule covers every event held up by an ou
         +tage, including a heavy user's new events that queue behind its
         + backlog after recovery. Its 30-second rule applies again once 
         +that user's backlog is clear.                                  
      14 +- A user's events reach us out of created_at order by at most a
         +bout 1 second.                                                 
      15  - created_at has at most 6 decimal places of seconds, and one w
          ithout a time zone is in UTC.
      16  - "The same body" in the brief means the same bytes.
      15 -- A post that repeats an event_id is a resend of the same event
         -, even if its body differs, and the first body stands.         
      16 -- A valid event is a JSON object under 1 MB with a non-empty ev
         -ent_id, user_id and created_at. Anything else gets a 400.      
      17 -- Our promise starts with our 2xx. A post we refused, during st
         -artup or with a 503 while Postgres restarts, carries none; if T
         -equity resends it after that user's later events went out, it a
         -rrives out of order.                                           
      17 +- A post that repeats an event_id is a resend of the same event
         +, even if its body differs.                                    
      18 +- A valid event is a JSON object under 1 MB with a non-empty ev
         +ent_id, user_id and created_at.

      104  ## 2026-11-19: A user waits while its oldest unsent event is u
           nder 1 second old
      105 
      106  The worker looks at each user's oldest unsent event by `create
           d_at`, then `seq`. If that event arrived less than 1 second ag

            a later event never goes out while an earlier one is held. Th
           e check runs before every send, so an earlier event that turns
            up mid-loop goes first once its second has passed.

           r 1 MB, and 503 to any database error except a refused created

           is logged with its reason. The server sets `keepAliveTimeout`
           to 65 seconds and `headersTimeout` to 66: with Node's 5-second
            default, our server can close an idle connection just as the
           load generator reuses it, and that post fails unanswered.

● Update(docs\decisions.md)
  ⎿  Added 1 line, removed 1 line
      158 
      159  Any 2xx means saved. Any other status, a refused connection an
           d a failed connect mean not saved; redirects are not followed.
            A not-saved event is retried after 20 ms, the wait doubling o
           n each failure in a row up to 5 seconds and going back to 20 m
           s after a success. A single 500 costs about 20 ms, a long fail
           ure backs off to one try every 5 seconds per user, and recover
           y is noticed within 5 seconds. A 4xx other than 429 is retried
            too, since skipping the event breaks exactly once.
      160

          -and a slower schedule for refused connections, which on localh
          -ost cost under 1 ms.                                          
      161 +Dropped: waits starting at 0.5 seconds (see the 10 ms entry), 
          +and a slower schedule for refused connections, which on localh
          +ost fail at once.

● Update(docs\decisions.md)
  ⎿  Added 1 line
      171  ## 2026-11-19: A send has no overall time limit
      172 
      173  The brief says a slow answer is still coming and a 200 means s
           aved, so a timeout and resend is the one way to save twice on
           an endpoint that is slow but alive. Sends use `http.request`,
           never `fetch`, which follows redirects and has a hidden 300-se
           cond limit; its `timeout` option stays unused, since it fires
           during a slow answer. Connecting has no timer: on localhost a
           connection is accepted or refused at once. An error before the
            socket connected means not sent, after it unknown.
      174 +                                                              
      175  An endpoint that never answers keeps that one user waiting; th
           e brief's table has no such endpoint.
      176 
      177  Dropped: a 60-second timeout, then a 120-second ceiling, each
           followed by a resend; and a 2-second connect timer, which a re
           used keep-alive socket never clears, so it would kill slow ans
           wers.

       96  ## The design is sized for an endpoint that answers in about 1
           0 ms
       97 
       98 -A user's events go out one at a time, so a heavy user can go n
          -o faster than one event per T = (a + o) ÷ (1 − p) + p × 20 ms.
          - Here a is the endpoint's answer time, o our own time per even
          -t, p the share of attempts answered 500, and 20 ms the first r
          -etry wait. o is about 5 ms, measured with pgbench on Docker De
          -sktop; the Linux run measures it again.

       99 
      100  Example: after a 5-minute outage, a user sending 30 a second o
           wes 30 × 600 = 18,000 events (9,000 from the outage, 9,000 arr
           iving after it) within 5 minutes, less up to 5 seconds before
           recovery is noticed. That allows T of about 16 ms, so with p a
           t 2%, a must be about 10 ms or less.

      125  `$1` holds the users in flight or paused, `$2` the free slots.

           sy users are left out before the limit, and the longest-waitin
           g user goes first. The index `events_pending ON events (user_i
           d, created_at, seq) INCLUDE (received_at) WHERE delivered_at I
           S NULL` holds unsent rows only.

      130 
      131 +                                                              
      132  ## created_at stays a string and is cast in SQL
      133 
      134  created_at never becomes a JavaScript `Date`, which keeps only
            milliseconds and would merge events that differ in the 4th to
            6th decimal. Intake checks it against an RFC 3339 pattern (`Z
           `, an offset such as `+05:30`, or no zone), cuts any fraction
           past 6 decimals with a log line, and passes it as `$1::timesta
           mptz`. Postgres runs with `timezone=UTC`, so no zone means UTC
           . Ordering stays in SQL, with ties broken by `seq`. A string t
           hat fails the pattern or the cast (SQLSTATE class 22, Postgres
           's data errors) gets a 400.

› Message from ecc:code-architect
  ## Architecture: file layout and interfaces (draft)

  ### File tree
  ```
  .gitattributes            LF rule
  package.json, package-lock.json   pg only; scripts: "test": "node --test"
  Dockerfile                one node:24.x.y-alpine image; npm ci --omit=dev; CMD
  set per service
  compose.yaml              postgres, api (CMD api.js), worker (CMD worker.js);
  pgdata volume
  db/init.sql               events table + events_pending index
  src/api.js                HTTP server on :8080, SIGTERM
  src/worker.js             poll loop, per-user loops, 429 pause, SIGTERM
  src/intake.js             validate(buf), insert(pool, parsed)
  src/send.js               post(agent, body) -> classification
  src/db.js                 Pool factory, error handlers, connect retry
  src/sql.js                readyUsers, oldestUnsent, markDelivered
  rig/compose.yaml          endpoint, loadgen, checker (host network)
  rig/Dockerfile            same Node image, rig code only
  rig/endpoint.js, rig/loadgen.js, rig/check.js
  test/intake.test.js, send.test.js, sql.test.js (need Postgres),
  rig/check.test.js
  ```
  `src/log.js` is skipped; use `console.error(JSON.stringify(...))`.

  ### Contracts
  **events table** (types for event_id and user_id are not given in
  decisions.md):
  - `event_id text PRIMARY KEY`
  - `user_id text NOT NULL`
  - `created_at timestamptz NOT NULL`
  - `body bytea NOT NULL`
  - `received_at timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now()`
  - `seq bigint GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY`
  - `delivered_at timestamptz` (nullable)
  - Index: `events_pending ON events (user_id, created_at, seq) INCLUDE
  (received_at) WHERE delivered_at IS NULL`.

  **Intake**: `POST :8080/events`, body is a JSON object.
  - 200: committed, or a repeat event_id (first body kept).
  - 400: not a JSON object, a missing or empty event_id, user_id or created_at,
  a failed RFC 3339 pattern, or SQLSTATE class 22.
  - 413: body over 1 MB.
  - 503: any other database error.
  - Every 400 is logged with its reason.

  **Worker to endpoint**: `POST :9000/events`, raw stored bytes, `Content-Type:
  application/json`, no redirects followed.
  - 2xx: saved.
  - 429: pause that user for max(1, Retry-After) seconds; the retry wait is
  unchanged.
  - Any other status: retry after a wait that starts at 20 ms, doubles per
  failure up to 5 s, and resets after a success.
  - Connection refused or lost before a status line: retry the same way.
  - Every response is drained with `res.resume()`.

  **Rig (proposals)**: all files are NDJSON, one object per line, `t` in epoch
  ms (number).
  - `loadgen.ndjson`: `{t_send, t_done, event_id, user_id, created_at, status}`.
  `status` is a number, or `null` plus `err` (string) when there was no answer.
  The checker counts a 2xx as promised.
  - `endpoint.ndjson`: `{t, event_id, user_id, created_at, bytes_sha256, mode,
  status, saved}`. `status` is the status the endpoint answered; `mode` is the
  mode in force. `saved` is a boolean. The body bytes are checked by hashing,
  and loadgen also logs the hash (add `body_sha256`).
  - Behaviour script, `script.json`: `{seed: int, steps: [{from_s, to_s, mode:
  "healthy"|"fail"|"down"|"slow"|"limit", p500?, latency_ms?, retry_after?}]}`.
  Inside a step, a PRNG seeded from `seed` picks each answer.
  - `modes.ndjson`: `{t, mode}` on every change, so the checker knows when the
  endpoint was healthy, failing or down.
  - Checker: reads the three files from `/data` and exits 0 or 1. It prints one
  line per check: `{check: "exactly_once"|"order"|"timing"|"intake_up"|"bytes",
  pass: bool, detail: string}`.

  ### Module interfaces
  - `intake.js`: `validate(buf)` returns `{ok, event_id, user_id, created_at}`
  or `{status, reason}`. `insert(pool, buf, fields)` returns `200 | 503 | 400`.
  - `send.js`: `post(agent, bodyBuf)` resolves `{kind: "saved"} | {kind:
  "pause", seconds} | {kind: "retry", status?, err?}`.
  - `sql.js`: `readyUsers(pool, excluded, limit)`, `oldestUnsent(pool, userId)`
  returns the row or null (hold test is in SQL), `markDelivered(pool, eventId)`.
  - `worker.js`: `nextWait(failures)` is pure; `runUser(deps, userId)` is
  injectable.

  Done per unit: `node --test` passes, using fixed inputs and the seeded script.
  The rig's `check.test.js` feeds hand-made logs, one violation per check.

  ### MISFITS
  - "The relay is written in Node.js 24, on Postgres 16": no column types,
  nullability or timestamptz for created_at.
  - "Intake answers 200 only after the commit": silent on non-POST methods,
  other paths and 404/405.
  - "Any 2xx means saved" and "A 429 pauses only the user": the pause is not in
  the poll's `$1` unless the exclusion list is in-memory only. This is fine, but
  it must be explicit.
  - CLAUDE.md "no files on disk" and the rig's NDJSON logs: this needs an
  exemption, since the rig is test code.
  - "A user waits while its oldest unsent event is under 1 second old" and the
  30 s rule: no conflict, but the poll's 200 ms and the 1 s hold set a floor of
  about 1.2 s.

  Skipped: I did not check the Docker Desktop caveats against the rig mounts, so
  the `/data` volume path is unchecked. Risk: the rig log schemas are untested
  guesses.

  the files once both are in.
