› Message from ecc:architect
  ## Draft for docs/architecture.md

  ### Parts

  ```
  Grading machine (Linux). Every container uses network_mode: host, so all share
  one localhost.

   [Tequity load generator]                    [Tequity endpoint :9000]
          | HTTP POST /events                         ^ HTTP POST /events (raw
  bytes,
          v                                           |  one keep-alive Agent)
   [intake API :8080, every address]          [worker: poll + up to 200 user
  loops]
          | SQL INSERT ... ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING     ^ SQL poll, next event,
  delivered_at
          v                                           |
   [Postgres 127.0.0.1:54329, volume pgdata] ---------+

  In tests, rig/compose.yaml stands in for Tequity (also host networking):
   [rig load generator] --HTTP POST--> :8080     [fake endpoint :9000] <--HTTP--
  worker
          | log file: events answered 2xx                | log file: events
  saved
          +------------------> [checker] <---------------+
  ```

  ### Life of one event

  1. **Receive.** A body over 1 MB gets 413. A 400, logged with its reason, goes
  to a body that is not a JSON object, lacks a non-empty event_id, user_id or
  created_at, or has a created_at that fails the RFC 3339 pattern. A fraction
  past 6 decimals is cut and logged. (Intake answers 200 only after the commit;
  created_at stays a string and is cast in SQL)
  2. **Insert.** `ON CONFLICT (event_id) DO NOTHING`, body as bytea. A refused
  cast (class 22) gets 400. A repeated event_id gets 200, the first body is
  kept, and a different body is logged. (event_id is the primary key, and a
  repeat keeps the first body) Any other database error, Postgres down included,
  gets 503 and the API stays up. (A database error or a failed loop never stops
  a process) A crash before the commit sends no answer and makes no promise. A
  crash after the commit but before the 200 leaves the row in place, and
  Tequity's resend gets 200.
  3. **Answer.** 200 after the commit. delivered_at is NULL.
  4. **Poll.** Each poll starts 200 ms after the last one finished. It picks
  users whose oldest unsent event (by created_at, then seq) is at least 1 second
  old by received_at. It skips users in flight or paused and takes the
  longest-waiting first, up to the free slots. (The poll picks ready users
  fairly, from a partial index) If that event is younger, the whole user waits.
  An earlier event that arrives within the second goes first. One that arrives
  after a later event was sent goes out late and is logged. (A user waits while
  its oldest unsent event is under 1 second old)
  5. **Send.** The in-memory in-flight set allows one loop per user. Before each
  send the loop reads the oldest unsent event again and checks the hold again.
  It then posts the stored bytes with `http.request`, with no timeout and no
  redirects. (One worker process, with delivered_at as the only record of
  progress; A send has no overall time limit)
  6. **Outcome.** The status line decides, and every response is drained. (The
  status line decides, and a connection lost before it means resend)
     - 2xx: saved. Go to 8.
     - 500, 3xx, or a 4xx other than 429: not saved. Retry after 20 ms, doubling
  up to 5 s. (Any 2xx means saved, and anything else is retried from 20 ms)
     - Refused or failed connect: same retry schedule. (same entry)
     - Connection lost before a status line: send again. (The status line
  decides…)
     - Slow answer: keep waiting. A warning is logged at 60 s and then each
  minute. (A send has no overall time limit; Each failed attempt is logged, and
  so is a send over 60 seconds)
     - 429: pause only this user for Retry-After seconds, at least 1. The retry
  wait does not grow. (A 429 pauses only the user who got it)
     - A thrown error is logged and counted as a failed attempt. (A database
  error or a failed loop never stops a process)
  7. **Crash or kill during a send.** The event is sent again on restart and may
  be saved twice. (A normal stop gives sends in flight 120 seconds)
  8. **Record.** delivered_at is committed before the user's next send. If the
  write fails, only the write is retried; the event is never resent for it. A
  crash after the 2xx but before the commit means the event is sent again and
  saved twice. (delivered_at is committed before the next send, and never causes
  a resend) The retry wait resets, and the loop goes back to step 5.
  9. **SIGTERM.** The worker stops polling and starting sends, lets in-flight
  sends and their writes finish, then exits. Compose kills it at 120 s. The API
  stops taking posts and lets its inserts commit. (A normal stop gives sends in
  flight 120 seconds)
  10. **Restart.** Services have `restart: unless-stopped`, and both processes
  retry Postgres. The worker logs its unsent count, and every row without
  delivered_at is sent again. (Startup waits for a healthy Postgres, and data
  lives in a named volume)

  ### MISFITS

  - **"A database error or a failed loop never stops a process" vs "delivered_at
  is committed before the next send…".** The loop's try/catch counts any error
  as a failed attempt. If a delivered_at write error after a 2xx reaches that
  catch, the retry resends an event that was already saved. Neither entry puts
  the write retry inside the catch.
  - **"A normal stop gives sends in flight 120 seconds" vs "delivered_at is
  committed before the next send…".** On SIGTERM the worker waits for a write
  that retries until it commits. If Postgres is down, the stop hangs until the
  120 s kill. On restart the saved event is resent and saved twice.
  - **"A send has no overall time limit" vs "The status line decides…".** The
  first calls an error after connect "unknown". The second calls a loss before
  the status line "not saved" and resends. The action is the same, but the two
  entries give the outcome different names.

  ### OPEN

  - OPEN: when the oldest event is still held, or none is left, does the loop
  end or sleep?
  - OPEN: during a 429 pause or a retry wait, does the loop sleep inside itself
  or end and leave a "paused" entry for the poll's `$1`?
  - OPEN: how the worker knows an event is late so it can log it.
  - OPEN: the wait between delivered_at write retries.
  - OPEN: whether event_id, user_id and created_at must be JSON strings.
  - OPEN: whether `stop_grace_period` of 120 s also applies to the API.
  - OPEN: the rig's log file format, and how the checker reads it.
  - OPEN: where API and worker logs go (stdout is assumed).

  Not checked: the word count is estimated, not counted. The two log-file arrows
  in the diagram are the task's wording; decisions.md does not describe them,
  hence the OPEN line.

    failed delivered_at write, or that write's error would cause a resend.
    architecture.md places the write retry inside the send step to make that
    explicit.
  - Two need your call, since they're decisions.md wording. I'll list them in my
    reply and change nothing.

  Doesn't fit together, needs your call

  - CLAUDE.md says "no files on disk", and the rig writes NDJSON logs. The plan
    treats the logs as test output. CLAUDE.md should say that rule covers the
    relay only.
  - decisions.md names one case two ways. A connection lost after connecting is
    "unknown" in the time-limit entry and "Not saved" in the status-line entry.
    The action is the same: resend.
  - SIGTERM while Postgres is down: the delivered_at write retries until the 120
    s kill, and the event is resent after restart. It's in the plan's review
    focus under U8, as reported but not blocking.

  Gaps I filled in architecture.md, not yet in decisions.md

  - event_id and user_id are text, and all three required fields must be
    non-empty JSON strings.
  - Any path other than POST /events gets 404.
  - When a user's oldest event is held, or none is left, the loop ends.
  - During a retry wait the loop sleeps in place and keeps its slot, which keeps
    the 20 ms retry maths. A 429 ends the loop and pauses the user.
  - An event that arrives after a later one was sent is spotted in SQL, never by
    comparing dates in JavaScript.
  - A failed delivered_at write retries on the same 20 ms to 5 s waits.

  Say which of these should become decisions.md entries before building starts.

  How to build

  - Subagent-driven (my recommendation): a fresh agent builds each unit and a
    reviewer checks it before the next starts. The relay units depend on each
    other's exact interfaces, and a mistake that ships breaks a pass condition.
  - Native: I build everything in this session, with one reviewer at the end.
    Cheaper, but nothing is checked until the end.

  I'll wait for your OK before building.

  **Exported interface:** `module.exports = { start }`
  - `start({ scenario, dataDir, port })` returns a Promise that resolves to `{
  port, close }`.
    - `port` is the port actually bound, so `port: 0` works.
    - `close()` is async. It clears every timer (step boundaries and pending
  slow answers), closes the server and closes all connections. It also resolves
  if the server is currently down.
  - When run directly, it reads the scenario from env `SCENARIO_FILE`, the log
  directory from `DATA_DIR` (default `/data`) and the port from `PORT` (default
  9000), then prints `endpoint listening on <port>`.

  **Where the contract was unclear, and what I chose:**
  - **When the random draw happens:** `next()` is drawn once for every `POST
  /events`, whatever the mode, after the body is read. A step's `p` matters only
  in fail and limit. So the answer sequence depends only on the order of
  requests.
  - **`latency_ms` in other modes:** it applies to any matched step, not only
  healthy and slow. Its default of 0 means nothing changes unless a scenario
  sets it.
  - **When the log line is written:** the `endpoint.ndjson` line is written
  right after the answer is sent, so `t` is the time of the answer. The mode is
  decided when the request body has been read.
  - **`users` on a down step:** a down step writes `users: null` in
  `modes.ndjson`, even if the scenario lists users, because down ignores `users`
  and hits everyone. Other steps write their `users` list, or null if they have
  none.
  - **Missing `retry_after`:** the `Retry-After` header is sent only when
  `retry_after` is not null, since there is no value to send otherwise.
  - **Body is valid JSON but not an object:** if it parses to null or a number,
  the three fields are logged as null.
  - **Unknown mode names** are treated like healthy, but the line logs the name
  as given.
  - **Answer body:** 200, 500, 429 and 404 answers all have an empty body.
  - **Requests that arrive during down:** down calls `server.close()`, which
  refuses new connections and, on Node 19 and later, also closes idle keep-alive
  sockets. A request that was already in flight when down began is still
  answered and logged. It gets the mode of whatever non-down step matches, or
  healthy. I did not kill those sockets.

  **Not checked:**
  - `close()` called while a down window is still open. The code handles it, but
  no test covers it.
  - Overlapping steps. They are untested beyond "first match wins".

  **Risk:** when down ends, the server listens again on the same port. If
  anything else takes that port during the outage, `listen` fails with an
  `error` event that nothing handles, and the process would crash.

  - c:\Users\dell\Documents\tequinty\second\rig\endpoint.test.js

      33  8. Crash or kill during 6 or 7: the event is resent on restart,

● The diagrams are committed as ed08ec2. Counting the diagram code,

› Message from ecc:typescript-reviewer
  Ran `node --test rig/endpoint.test.ts`: 8/8 pass. The typecheck did not run,
  because typescript is not installed here and `npx tsc` fetched an unrelated
  package. I did not run `npm run typecheck`.

  1. rig/endpoint.ts:94 and :90. A string `retry_after` with a control
  character, for example "a\nb", makes `res.writeHead` throw `ERR_INVALID_CHAR`
  inside the timer callback. That is an uncaught exception that kills the
  endpoint, and the request is never logged. I reproduced this. The plan allows
  a deliberately broken header string, so a scenario can hit it. Certain. Fix:
  wrap the writeHead in try/catch and fall back to a 429 with no header, or
  validate the string in `parseStep`.

  2. rig/endpoint.ts:119 and :124. `server.listen(boundPort)` runs in a timer.
     - If two `down` steps overlap, the second `listen` throws
  `ERR_SERVER_ALREADY_LISTEN` synchronously and crashes the process. Likely, not
  run.
     - If the re-bind fails (EADDRINUSE), the error is swallowed. The
  `server.once('error', reject)` handler is never removed after a successful
  start, so it absorbs it. The endpoint stays down while modes.ndjson already
  says `healthy`. Likely.
     - Fix: guard with `if (!server.listening)`, and replace the startup
  `reject` handler with one that logs and exits.

  3. rig/endpoint.ts:117-120. The `healthy` mode line is written at `to_s`
  before `listen` finishes, and it is also written when another step still
  covers that moment. The checker's `intake_up` and `timing` checks could
  misread this. Medium-likely. Fix: write the line from the `listen` callback,
  and only if no other step is active.

  4. rig/endpoint.ts:48-49 and :110-121. Startup truncates the log files and
  arms the step timers before `listen` succeeds. If `listen` fails with
  EADDRINUSE, the promise rejects but the timers keep running, and the
  half-started run has already wiped the previous logs. Fairly sure. Fix: call
  `listen` first, then truncate the logs and arm the timers in its callback.

  5. rig/endpoint.ts:95 and :99. `appendFileSync` is unguarded, so a missing or
  full `dataDir` throws in a timer and crashes the process. `t` is taken after
  the latency delay, so it is response time, not receive time. Check whether the
  checker's `timing` check expects receive time. Medium-low on the crash,
  unsure on `t`. Fix: capture `t` at `end` and decide whether to catch the write
  error.

  6. rig/types.ts:110-111. A `retry_after` that is neither number nor string
  (null, boolean) is silently dropped, while every other field throws. The same
  function does not check `from_s < to_s`, `0 <= p <= 1` or `latency_ms >= 0`. A
  typo then turns into a healthy endpoint with no error. Certain. Fix: throw on
  a wrong type and add the three range checks.

  7. rig/types.ts:104 and :114-117. `mode as Mode` is a cast after a runtime
  check, and `users` is `any[]` from `Array.isArray`, so `any` leaks into
  `Step`. Both are small and the runtime checks are real. Fix: use a type guard
  `isMode(x): x is Mode` and `users.filter((u): u is string => ...)`.

  8. rig/endpoint.ts:149-153. `main` does not check `PORT`, so `Number("abc")`
  gives NaN and `listen(NaN)` fails oddly. The scenario `JSON.parse` has no
  message when the file is bad. Low. Fix: `Number.isInteger(port)` check.

  9. rig/endpoint.test.ts:125-129. Flaky timing. `ev(1)` for u0001 must arrive
  before the 0.1 s step start, so a slow first connection, JIT or a loaded CI
  machine makes it a 500 and fails the test. The `down` test (:72-77) has about
  150 ms of margin on each side. Fix: widen the windows, for example `from_s`
  0.5, or use users that are not in the step for the pre-step posts.

  10. rig/endpoint.test.ts: test gaps.
      - `parseScenario` and `rng` have no tests at all, although `parseScenario`
  is the scenario-file boundary.
      - `down` is not checked for ignoring `users` or for its mode lines
  (`users` null).
      - There is no test for `close()` during a down window or with a latency
  timer pending. I think `close()` is correct, but nothing shows it.
      - There is no `limit` with `p` test, and no `retry_after` string test,
  which would have caught item 1.
      - Test temp dirs are never removed.

  11. rig/endpoint.ts:84. Fine and deterministic only for serial requests. The
  draw happens in arrival order, so concurrent load from loadgen gives seeded
  chances but not a repeatable sequence. Acceptable. This is a note, not a fix.

  Checked and fine:
  - Step matching (first match, `down` excluded, null `user_id` never matches a
  `users` list).
  - The single draw per request.
  - Seed handling.
  - The 404 path (no logging, body dumped by Node).
  - `close()` resolving when already down, and `boundPort` reuse for port 0.
  - Aborted client requests: IncomingMessage only emits 'error' when a listener
  exists, so no crash. I did not run this.

  Not over-built. The only extras are `Endpoint`/`StartOptions` and
  `parseScenario`, which are needed.

  Skipped: the typecheck (typescript is not installed), tsconfig/package.json
  settings other than a read-through (no problems seen), and loadgen/check.

● The review found real problems. My calls:

  - 1. A retry_after string with a control character crashes the endpoint:
    fixing. parseScenario will accept only printable header text.
  - 2. A second listen throws on overlapping down steps, and a failed re-bind
    error is swallowed: fixing. listen and close get guards, and the startup
    error handler is removed once the server is up, so a later failure crashes
    loudly.
  - 3. Overlapping steps make modes.ndjson wrong: fixing. parseScenario will
    refuse steps that overlap in time and in users. That also rules out
    overlapping downs.
  - 4. Logs are wiped and timers start before listen succeeds: fixing. Both now
    wait for the listen callback.
  - 5. A failed log write crashes the process: keeping that, since a rig that
    can't log must stop loudly. t stays the time of the answer, because that's
    when the endpoint saved.
  - 6. A wrong retry_after type is silently dropped, and there are no range
    checks: fixing. Both now throw.
  - 7. Two casts at the boundary: fixing, with type guards.
  - 8. PORT isn't validated: fixing.
  - 9. Two tests have tight timing windows: fixing, by widening them.
  - 10. Test gaps: fixing. New rig/types.test.ts covers parseScenario and rng.

    with p and a string header. Temp folders get cleaned up.

  **Exported interface (`rig/loadgen.ts`):**
  ```ts
  export interface Post { at_ms: number; body: Buffer; event_id: string | null;
  user_id: string | null; created_at: string | null; valid: boolean; }
  export function plan(scenario: Scenario): Post[];
  export async function run(opts: { scenario: Scenario; dataDir: string; url:
  string }): Promise<void>;
  ```
  When run directly it uses `import.meta.main`, and reads `SCENARIO_FILE`
  through `parseScenario`. `DATA_DIR` defaults to `/data` and `INTAKE_URL` to
  `http://localhost:8080/events`. It throws if `SCENARIO_FILE` is not set.

  **Contract points that were unclear, and what I chose:**
  - **Post count and timing:** there are exactly `round(rate * duration_s)`
  slots, at `at_ms = floor(n*1000/rate)`. Each extra uses up a slot rather than
  adding a post. That is how no 1-second window ever holds more than `rate`
  posts.
  - **Extras:** each slot makes one draw against the extras' chances added
  together, so the five outcomes exclude each other. If the extras add up to
  more than 1, the later ones get less than their stated chance.
  - **`repeat` and `diff_body`:** these pick from the last 10 new valid events
  of any user, not from the slot's user. That keeps them "a little later", and
  they still follow the heavy/light split on average. `diff_body` changes only
  `data.email`.
  - **`out_of_order`:** a new event is created for the user of the most recent
  new event. It takes that event's earlier slot, and the older event moves to
  the current slot, so the gap is at most 1000 ms. If there is no recent new
  event within 1 s, or that event was already moved, the slot becomes a normal
  new event. A moved event cannot be moved again, so no gap grows past 1 s. The
  new event's `created_at` comes from its original slot time (with the per-user
  guard), so it can be later than its send time. That is intended.
  - **Invalid bodies:** fields an invalid body lacks are null. "Empty event_id"
  logs `event_id: ""`, not null. "created_at yesterday" logs `created_at:
  "yesterday"`. Invalid posts do not move the user's `created_at` guard.
  - **Event ids:** made of 32 hex digits from the seeded rng, in 8-4-4-4-12
  form. I did not set uuid version or variant bits.
  - **`err` values:** the error code from Node (for example `ECONNREFUSED`), or
  its message, or `timeout`. The 10 s limit is a plain timer over the whole
  request, not the socket idle timeout. One line is logged per post.
  - **Log writes:** each line is appended to `loadgen.ndjson` as its answer
  arrives. The file was truncated at start.

  The 10 s timeout path has no test, to keep test runs short. I did not run
  anything against the real intake API.

  npx tsc --noEmit

  Final output: only npm's "New major version of npm available! 11.19.1 ->
  12.2.3" notice, then exit 0. No type errors in any file, including the other
  agents' files at that moment.

  **Command-line run:**
  - With DATA_DIR pointing at an empty directory, `node rig/check.ts` printed
  five `nothing to judge: ...` failure lines and exited 1.
  - With `--wait` and a scenario file where drain_s is 2, it waited 2 s before
  grading.

  **Exported interface:** `check(dataDir: string): CheckResult[]` is the only
  export. It returns the five results in the order exactly_once, order, timing,
  intake_up, bytes. A line that is not JSON, or has the wrong shape, throws an
  error naming the file and line, for example `endpoint.ndjson:1: malformed
  line: ...`.

  **Contract points that were unclear, and what I chose:**
  1. Timing case (b) could shorten the deadline. If the user's last recovery is
  more than 270 s before the event's promise time, "recovery + 300 s" is earlier
  than "promise time + 30 s". I take the later of the two, so case (b) only
  ever extends a deadline.
  2. Timing case (a) when the endpoint never recovers. If there is no recovery
  at or after the promise time, the deadline has no limit. The event passes if
  it was saved at all, and fails if it was never saved.
  3. What "earlier promised event" means in case (b). I read it as promised
  earlier, by the lowest t_done of its 2xx posts, not as an earlier created_at.
  It must also have been promised by the time of the recovery and still be
  unsaved then.
  4. Mode timeline. Before the first modes.ndjson line every user counts as
  healthy. That starting healthy period is not a recovery. Mode lines are sorted
  by t, with ties kept in file order.
  5. Saved lines with a null event_id. exactly_once counts them as saved but
  never promised, so they fail it.
  6. created_at format. I accept YYYY-MM-DDTHH:MM:SS, with an optional fraction
  of 1 to 6 digits, then Z, ±HH:MM, or no zone (read as UTC). A saved event
  whose created_at cannot be read fails order. order compares each save with the
  user's previous save.
  7. relay_stopped windows. A window starts at relay_stopped and ends just
  before the next relay_running. A stop with no later relay_running runs to the
  end of the logs. Outside the windows, a valid post that got no answer at all
  fails intake_up.

  I did not check made-up dates such as Feb 31: JavaScript's Date rolls them
  over instead of rejecting them. The timing check is near O(n log n) per user
  plus a pass over each user's events per recovery, but I have not run it on a
  full-size log.

› Message from ecc:typescript-reviewer
  I read rig/check.ts, the plan, README and the endpoint writer. I did not run
  the tests, and I did not read check.test.ts beyond its test names.

  1. **check.ts:171-173, timing passes late events (vacuous pass).** The
  extension uses `rb`, the last recovery at or before the save time `s`. It
  treats any earlier event unsaved at `rb` as proof of a backlog. This breaks in
  two ways.
     - Backlog already cleared. A recovery happens at R. Event f, promised
  earlier, is unsaved at R and is saved at R+10 s. Event e is promised at R+100
  s and saved at R+200 s, with the endpoint healthy throughout.
  `blockedSince(R)` is f's time, which is less than `e.at`, so e's deadline
  becomes R+300 s and e passes. README line 13 says the 30 s rule applies again
  once the backlog is clear, so the correct deadline is R+130 s and e should
  fail.
     - Later outage. e is promised at 0 and the endpoint is healthy from 100 s.
  Another earlier event f is also unsaved. A second outage runs 500-900 s, with
  a recovery at 900 s. e is saved at 1000 s. The extension lifts e's deadline to
  900+300, so it passes, although e was already late at 400 s while healthy.
     - Sure: 70% for the first case (the plan wording "unsaved when the endpoint
  last recovered" is literal, so the plan itself may need a fix), 50% for the
  second.
     - Fix: extend only when an earlier event of the same user is still unsaved
  at `e.at` (`f.at < e.at && savedAt(f) > e.at`), and take the recovery that
  precedes `e.at`, not `s`.

  2. **check.ts:198-209, intake_up passes with zero posts judged.** Every post
  can fall inside a `relay_stopped` window. An open window with no
  `relay_running` extends to Infinity, so a relay that crashed early excuses
  every later post. Example: `relay_stopped` at t=0 with no `relay_running`,
  then 500 posts that all got 500 or a refusal. `judged` is 0, so the check
  reports "0 posts answered correctly" and passes. A relay that never starts
  therefore passes.
     - Sure: 85%.
     - Fix: return `nothing(...)` when `judged === 0`.

  3. **check.ts:213-224, bytes passes with zero comparisons.** `saved` is
  non-empty, but no saved event is in `promised`, so `judged` is 0 and the check
  passes. It also passes if the relay saves events the loadgen never posted.
  exactly_once catches that case, but bytes should not pass alone.
     - Sure: 90%.
     - Fix: `if (judged === 0) return nothing('bytes', ...)`.

  4. **check.ts:147, check.ts:162, check.ts:171, timing lets a short healthy
  tail count as a recovery.** A healthy period with `end === Infinity` counts as
  a recovery however short it is. Example: the logs show healthy from 1000 s to
  the end, with the last mode line at 1005 s. That is accepted as a recovery,
  and the deadline is 1300 s. With a short drain, the event is "never saved" and
  fails, which is safe. If it is saved at 1290 s, it passes even though the
  endpoint may have failed again right after the log ended. Lenient only when
  logs are cut short.
     - Sure: 40%. The plan comment says this is intended.
     - Fix: none needed if the drain wait is long enough; otherwise require 30 s
  of healthy time before the last log line.

  5. **check.ts:80, check.ts:83, promised by first 2xx.** If an invalid post
  gets a 2xx, it is never promised, and a saved copy is reported as "never
  promised". This is correct. A valid post with `event_id` null and a 2xx is
  silently dropped from `promised`. If the relay answers 2xx to a valid post
  with no `event_id` and saves it, exactly_once still catches it as "(no
  event_id)", so the verdict is right. The reverse case is a gap: the relay
  answers 2xx and saves nothing, and exactly_once cannot see it. Minor.
     - Sure: 50%.
     - Fix: count valid 2xx posts with a null `event_id` as offences.

  6. **check.ts:53-63, micros accepts impossible stamps.** `Date.UTC` rolls over
  out-of-range fields, so `2025-13-45T99:99:99Z` parses as a real time instead
  of failing. A mis-ordered pair with such stamps could pass. Fractions with
  more than 6 digits are rejected, which is loud, and that is fine. An offset
  like `+99:99` is also accepted.
     - Sure: 60%.
     - Fix: range-check month, day, hour, minute and second, or round-trip the
  result through `new Date(wholeMs).toISOString()`.

  7. **check.ts:37-40, a missing file counts as empty.** If `endpoint.ndjson` or
  `loadgen.ndjson` is missing, the checks fail as "nothing to judge", which is
  safe. A missing `modes.ndjson` makes timing and intake_up stricter, which is
  also safe, but it hides a rig bug that is not reported loudly.
     - Sure: 60%.
     - Fix: throw when `modes.ndjson` is missing.

  Checked and fine:
  - Strict `<=` in order, plus the per-user ordering by endpoint `t` with a
  stable sort.
  - Date-free microsecond arithmetic using BigInt.
  - Malformed lines throw with file and line.
  - `s === Infinity` avoids a NaN lateness.
  - The endpoint logs `t` at save time, so sorting by `t` matches the order the
  events were saved.
  - No `any` and no unsafe casts. The only cast is `MODES as readonly string[]`
  for `.includes`, which is harmless.

  Not checked: the check.test.ts contents, and whether the endpoint writes
  `users: null` for `down` and when steps overlap per user.

  else. Reading check.ts before fixing.

› Message from ecc:typescript-reviewer
  I ran rig/loadgen.test.ts in the container: 8 of 8 pass. The plan logic holds
  up: it is deterministic, each user's created_at strictly increases outside
  deliberate swaps, the 1-second grid never exceeds `rate`, and the out-of-order
  swaps stay within 1 s. I found no `any`, and the only unsafe casts are the
  test's `as LoadgenLine` and `as AddressInfo`, which are minor. I did not run
  tsc or eslint, and I did not run the failure paths below.

  1. rig/loadgen.ts:109-143, 147-148. A synchronous throw in `send` is not
  handled.
     - What throws: `request()` throws synchronously on a bad URL or an `https:`
  URL (the plain-http Agent mismatches). `appendFileSync` can throw too.
     - Effect: the post gets no LoadgenLine, `Promise.all` rejects at once,
  `agent.destroy()` is skipped, and the remaining timers keep firing until the
  process dies.
     - Confidence: about 85% on the behaviour, but it only bites on
  misconfiguration or a full disk.
     - Fix: wrap the `request()` call in try/catch that calls `finish(null,
  e.message)`, and move `agent.destroy()` into a `finally`.

  2. rig/loadgen.ts:63-72. A repeat or diff_body can be sent before the original
  event.
     - Cause: a new event becomes `lastNew` and goes into `recent`. A repeat or
  diff_body of it at n+1, then an out_of_order swap at n+2, moves the original
  to slot n+2.
     - Effect: the "another body" copy arrives first and wins the 2xx, which
  contradicts "the same event again".
     - Confidence: about 80% on the mechanism, but the checker may still pass
  either way.
     - Fix: set `lastNew = -1` in the repeat/diff branch if `old ===
  posts[lastNew]`. The tests don't catch this because they only count first
  sends by event_id.

  3. rig/loadgen.ts:134-141. Status is lost when the response fails after
  headers or on timeout.
     - Cause: after a 2xx header, a stalled or aborted body logs `status: null`
  with `err`.
     - Effect: the checker's `exactly_once` is based on 2xx answers, so a saved
  event looks never answered.
     - Confidence: about 70% on the mechanism, but it is rare in practice
  because the intake replies with an empty body.
     - Fix: keep `res.statusCode` in a variable and pass it to `finish(status,
  err)` on res error or timeout.
     - Not a bug: every other path I traced logs exactly one line. The `done`
  guard covers 'error' plus 'response', timeout followed by destroy-induced
  errors, and a res error after headers.

  4. rig/loadgen.test.ts, whole file. The `run` failure paths and the schedule
  have no test. Missing cases:
     - timeout;
     - a response aborted after headers;
     - a server that answers slowly while later posts still go out on time. This
  is the "does not wait for answers" requirement, and nothing proves it.
     - Only the success path and ECONNREFUSED are covered.
     - Confidence: 95%.
     - Fix: add a server that never answers, with `LIMIT_MS` made injectable or
  the test using a fake timer. Add a server that destroys the socket mid-body.
  Add a slow server and assert the spread of `t_send`.

  5. rig/loadgen.test.ts:198. `sends.at(-1) - sends[0] >= 900` is a timing
  assertion with only a 50 ms margin. A late first timer on a loaded CI box can
  shrink the spread and fail the test.
     - Confidence: about 60%.
     - Fix: compare each `t_send` against `t0 + at_ms - tolerance`, or relax to
  >= 500.

  6. rig/loadgen.ts:144-146. All timers are created up front, at one per post
  (30k or more for long scenarios). If the event loop stalls, late timers fire
  together, so the real send rate can briefly exceed `rate` inside a 1-second
  window even though the plan is on the grid.
     - Confidence: about 50% that it matters. It is probably acceptable for the
  rig.
     - Fix: none needed unless the checker ever grades the real send windows.

      67    const back = new Date(wholeMs);
      68    if (back.getUTCMonth() + 1 !== Number(mo) || back.getUTCDate(
          ) !== Number(d) || back.getUTCHours() !== Number(h)
      69      || back.getUTCMinutes() !== Number(mi) || back.getUTCSecond
          s() !== Number(s)) return null;

  **`run` does start the dependencies and wait for loadgen.**
  - It started endpoint, then loadgen, and check ran only after loadgen
  completed.
  - Wall time was 160 s (01:52:51 to 01:55:31, and 01:55:59 to 01:58:38). That
  is 120 s of load plus the 35 s drain plus the build.
  - check graded all 12,000 posts, so loadgen had finished before it read the
  logs.
  - The exit code is the checker's. No alternative to `run` was needed.

  **Line counts** (now read from the volume with `docker compose -f
  rig/compose.yaml run --rm --no-deps check sh -c 'wc -l /data/*.ndjson'`):

  | File | Lines | Contents |
  |---|---|---|
  | loadgen.ndjson | 12,000 | all `status: null`, `err: "ECONNREFUSED"` |
  | endpoint.ndjson | 0 | |
  | modes.ndjson | 1 | the startup "healthy" line |

  The sends spread over 119.8 s and the slowest post took 68 ms.

  **Stale files:** endpoint truncates endpoint.ndjson and modes.ndjson when it
  binds, and loadgen truncates loadgen.ndjson at start. check starts only after
  loadgen exits 0, so it never reads an old file. I tested this by putting a
  fake `{"stale":true}` line in each file before a run; afterwards `grep -c
  stale` gave 0 for all three. Running `down` after each run means the next run
  starts a new endpoint container, so it truncates again.

  **Unit tests and tsc** (your exact `docker run ... sh -c 'node --test; npx
  --no-install tsc --noEmit'`):
  - `node --test`: tests 52, pass 52, fail 0, exit 0.
  - `tsc --noEmit`: no errors, exit 0.

  **What I changed from your spec, and why:**
  1. **Named volume instead of the bind mount.**
     - The first bind-mount run was clean. Runs 2 and 3 still printed five
  FAILs, but intake_up failed for the wrong reason: run 3 had 4,181
  ECONNREFUSED, 1,240 ETIMEDOUT and 6,579 timeouts.
     - In those runs the sends spread over 223 s instead of 120 s, and posts
  with a 10 s limit took up to 133 s, while loadgen used 3% CPU. Its event loop
  was blocked, not busy.
     - The cause is loadgen's `appendFileSync` for each line. I timed 12,000
  appends in the pinned image: 137 ms in container /tmp, 389,721 ms on the
  Windows bind mount.
     - I changed the mount to `data:/data` with a top-level `volumes: data:`,
  which creates the volume `rig_data`. Two runs after that were clean.
     - The cost is that the logs are no longer visible in rig/data on the host;
  read them through the volume as shown above. I deleted my test output in
  rig/data, so that path is now unused.
     - The bind mount is only slow on Docker Desktop; on the graders' Linux it
  would be fine. If you want it back there, it is a one-line change.
  2. **Added `pull_policy: build`.** Without it, `run` printed "pull access
  denied for outbox-rig" warnings and would keep using an old image after a rig
  file changed. With it, the image rebuilds from cache on every run (about 1 s).
  I confirmed a newly added rig file shows up in the next run without a manual
  build.

  **For later:**
  - docs/plan.md still says to run scenarios with `up --exit-code-from check`,
  and the "Scenario files" section doesn't mention the named volume. I left both
  because you said not to edit existing files.
  - `down` takes about 10 s because node runs as PID 1 and ignores SIGTERM.
  `init: true` would fix that; I didn't add it.
  - I only ran the healthy scenario. The other eight passed parseScenario but I
  have not run them end to end.
